\(\displaystyle \| B_t (v) - B_s (v) \|_{\mathcal{A}} \leqslant {\| \Xi \|_{\mathcal{G} (L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes V)}} | t - s |^{1 / 2} \| v \|_V, \qquad t, s \geqslant 0, v \in V, \) (21)

so \(B (v) \in C (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{A})\) (here \(C (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{A})\) is the space of continuous maps from \(\mathbb{R}_+\) to \(\mathcal{A}\)). Note that \(B \in \operatorname{Hom} (\Lambda V, C (\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{A}))\): this in particular implies that \(B_{t_1}, \ldots, B_{t_n}\) is a compatible family and that \(B = (B_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}_+}\) is a Gaussian process with continuous trajectories. We have that \(B_0 (v) = 0\),

\(\displaystyle \omega (B_t (v)) = 0, \qquad \omega (B_t (v) B_s (w)) = \langle v, C w \rangle (t \wedge s), \qquad t, s \geqslant 0, \quad v, w \in V,\)

where \(C\) is the correlation of the Grassmann white noise \(\Xi\). Increments of \(B\) are independent and higher order moments can be computed via Wick's rule (17). Note in particular that

\(\displaystyle \sup_{0 \leqslant t \leqslant T} \| B_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} < \infty, \) (22)

for all \(T > 0\). Let us remark here that properties (21) and (22) are very different from the pathwise properties of the commutative (bosonic) Brownian motion where the random variable realizing Brownian motion is unbounded both with respect the probability space and in time (see Remark 18 for other observations on this topic).

3Finite dimensional SDEs

We want to study simple SDEs taking values in \(\Lambda V\) as far as it is needed for the purpose of stochastic quantization, that is with additive white noise. We refrain to undertake here a general study of Grassmann SDEs, in particular no stochastic calculus will be needed below. It seems possible to devise such a calculus but we leave it for a future work. More precisely in this section we want to study SDEs driven by an additive Brownian motion \(B_t\) when the linear space \(V\) is finite dimensional. For any interval \(I \subseteq \mathbb{R}\) we denote by \(C^0 (I ; \mathcal{G} (V))\) the space of continuous function from \(I\) to \(\mathcal{G} (V)\).

Definition 23. Let \(F \in \mathcal{L} (V, \Lambda V)\) and assume that \(F (v)\) is odd for all \(v \in V\), let \(\Psi_0 \in \mathcal{G} (V)\) be a random variable compatible and independent of the Brownian motion \(B \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}_+ ; \mathcal{G} (V))\). For \(T > 0\), \(\Psi \in C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{G} (V))\) is a solution in \([0, T]\) to the (additive) SDE driven by \(B\) with drift \(F\) and initial condition \(\Psi_0\), if,

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t (v) = \Psi_0 (v) + \int_0^t \Psi_s (F (v)) \mathrm{d} s + B_t (v), \qquad t \in [0, T], v \in V, \) (23)

where the integral with respect to the variable \(s\) is understood in Bochner's sense with values in \(\mathcal{A}\).

Remark 24. Note that we do not require that \(\Psi_t, \Psi_s\) are compatible for \(t \neq s\), in particular

\(\displaystyle C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{G} (V)) \neq \operatorname{Hom} (\Lambda V ; C ([0, T], \mathcal{A})),\)

nor we require any compatibility of \(\Psi\) and \(B\). It will turn out that such compatibility holds in fact for the unique solution of (23), but it is not necessary to put such restriction to formulate the notion of solution.

3.1The Grassmann Ornstein–Uhlenbeck motion

In this section we introduce the Grassmann analog of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process, that is when \(F (v) = A v\) with \(A : V \rightarrow V\) is a linear operator on \(V\). In this case we can write down an explicit formula for the solution to equation (23) extended to \(t \geqslant 0\).

Proposition 25. Assume that \(A : V \rightarrow V\) and \(F (v) = A v\). The unique solution to equation (23) is given by

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t (v) = \Psi_0 (e^{A t} v) + \Xi (\mathbb{I}_{[0, t]} (\cdot) e^{A (t - \cdot)} v), \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}_+, \) (24)

where \(\Xi\) is the Grassmann Gaussian noise related with the Brownian motion \(B\) (see Definition 22 and the discussion that follows it).

Proof. Let \(h (t, v) \in L^2 (\mathbb{R}; V)\) be given by

\(\displaystyle h (t, v) (s) := \mathbb{I}_{[0, t]} (s) e^{A (t - s)} v, \qquad s \in \mathbb{R}.\)

Then (24) reads \(\Psi_t (v) = \Psi_0 (e^{A t} v) + \Xi (h (t, v))\). An explicit computation using that

\(\displaystyle h (t, v) (s) -\mathbb{I}_{[0, t]} (s) v = \int_0^t A h (r, v) (s) \mathrm{d} r = \int_0^t h (r, A v) (s) \mathrm{d} r, \qquad s \in \mathbb{R},\)

in \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}; V)\) gives

\(\displaystyle \Xi (h (t, v)) = B_t (v) + \int_0^t \Xi (h (r, A v)) \mathrm{d} r.\)

Moreover, by the definition of exponential of a matrix, \(\partial_t \Psi_0 (e^{A t} v) = \Psi_0 (e^{A t} A v)\) so

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t (v) = \Psi_0 (v) + \int_0^t \Psi_0 (e^{A r} A v) \mathrm{d} r + B_t (v) + \int_0^t \Xi (h (r, A v)) \mathrm{d} r\)
\(\displaystyle = \Psi_0 (v) + B_t (v) + \int_0^t \Psi_r (A v) \mathrm{d} r\)

as required by equation (23) for our assumption on \(F\).\(\Box\)

Remark 26. In particular this shows that if \((\Psi_0, \Xi)\) is a Grassmann Gaussian process then also the solution \(\Psi\) to the SDE (23), is a Grassmann Gaussian process compatible with \((\Psi_0, \Xi)\). In order to prove this fact it is sufficient to prove that any product of the form

\(\displaystyle \omega (\Psi_{t_1} (v_1) \cdots \Psi_{t_{2 k}} (v_{2 k})) \) (25)

can be computed using the products (weighted with a suitable sign due to the anti-commutation of \(\Psi\)) of the covariance \(\omega (\Psi_{t_i} (v_i) \Psi_{t_j} (v_j))\) for any \(i, j = 1, \ldots, 2 k\). This property of the expectation (25) follows from the fact that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} \omega ((\Psi_{t_1} (v_1) - \Psi_0) \cdots (\Psi_{t_{2 k}} (v_n) - \Psi_0)) = \omega (\Xi (h (t_1, v_1)) \cdots \Xi (h (t_{2 k}, v_{2 k}))) =\\ = \sum_{\sigma} (- 1)^{\sigma} \prod_{i = 1}^k \int_0^{\min (t_i, t_j)} \langle e^{A (t_i - s)} v_{\sigma (2 i - 1)}, C e^{A (t_j - s)} v_{\sigma (2 i)} \rangle_V \mathrm{d} s \end{array}\)

where \(C\) is the covariance of \(\Xi\). Using the fact that \(\Psi_0\) is a Gaussian random variable independent of \(\Xi\), and so \(\Psi_0 (e^{A (t - s)} v)\) is also a Gaussian random variable independent of \(\Xi\), we obtain the Gaussian behavior of \(\Psi_t\). The compatibility of \(\Psi_t\) with \((\Psi_0, \Xi)\) follows from the fact that \(\Psi\) is a linear function of \((\Psi_0, \Xi) .\)

Let us now study the family of random variables

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}} (v) = \Xi (e^{A (t - \cdot)} v), \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}.\)

In the next proposition, we will show that (under some specified assumptions on \(A\)) this represents the stationary solution to the SDE (24).

Proposition 27. Assume that all eigenvalues of \(A\) have strictly negative real part less or equal than \(- \lambda_A\), where \(\lambda_A > 0\). Then, we have \(\sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| \Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} < + \infty\), furthermore for any \(G \in \Lambda V\) and any \(t \in \mathbb{R}\) we get

\(\displaystyle \omega (G (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)) = \omega (G (X^A)), \) (26)

where \(X^A \in \mathcal{G} (V)\) is a Gaussian random variable with covariance \(C_A\) given by

\(\displaystyle C_A := \int^{\infty}_0 e^{A^{\mathrm{T}} s} C e^{A s} \mathrm{d} s. \) (27)

where \(A^{\mathrm{T}}\) denotes the transpose matrix.

Remark 28. Expression (26) in particular shows the independence on \(t\) of the expectation \(\omega (G (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t))\), expressing the stationarity of \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t\).

Proof of Proposition 27. We have easily by the definition of \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t\):

\(\displaystyle \| \Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \lesssim \| \mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} (\cdot) e^{A (t - \cdot)} \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R})} \lesssim \frac{1}{\lambda_A},\)

where \(\lesssim\) stands for inequality modulo some appropriate positive constant. The random variable \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t\) is Gaussian so it is completely characterized (in term of moments) by its covariance. Note that the covariance of \(\Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}}\) can be computed as follows

\(\displaystyle \omega (\Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}} (v) \Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}} (w)) = \omega (\Xi (\mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} (\cdot) e^{A (t - \cdot)} v) \Xi (\mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} (\cdot) e^{A (t - \cdot)} w)) =\)
\(\displaystyle = \langle \mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} (\cdot) e^{A (t - \cdot)} v, C\mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} (\cdot) e^{A (t - \cdot)} w \rangle = \int^0_{- \infty} \langle e^{- A^{\mathrm{T}} \tau} v, C e^{- A \tau} w \rangle \mathrm{d} \tau = \langle v, C_A w \rangle\)

with \(C_A\) given by (27). The appearance of the transposition is due to the fact that \(V\) is a real pre-Hilbert space.\(\Box\)

Let us point out that

\(\displaystyle A^{\mathrm{T}} C_A + C_A A = - C \) (28)

by a simple integration by parts applied to (27).

Remark 29. Suppose that \(A\) commutes with \(C\), then we have that \(\Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}}\) is for all \(t \in \mathbb{R}\) a Grassmann Gaussian process with correlation \(C_A = - (A + A^{\mathrm{T}})^{- 1} C\) (the operator \(A\) is invertible since all its eigenvalues has strictly negative real part).

3.2Existence and uniqueness for general drift

Theorem 30. For any \(\Psi_0 \in \mathcal{G} (V)\) compatible with a given Brownian motion \(B\), there exists \(T > 0\) such that equation (23) admits a unique solution \(\Psi \in C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{G} (V))\). Moreover \(\Psi\) is compatible with \(\Psi_0 \oplus B\).

Proof. We are going to construct a solution via Picard's iteration. Let \(\Psi^0_t = \Psi_0\) for all \(t \geqslant 0\) and define the \(n + 1\)-th Picard's iteration by

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t^{n + 1} (v) = \Psi_0 (v) + \int_0^t \Psi_s^n (F (v)) \mathrm{d} s + B_t (v), \qquad t \in [0, T],\)

for any \(v \in V\). Then \(\Psi^0 \in C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{G} (V))\) and since \(\Psi_0\) is compatible with \(B\) we have that, for all \(t \geqslant 0\), \(\Psi^1_t\) belong to the Grassmann algebra generated by \(\Psi_0\) and \(B\) in \(\mathcal{A}\) and there \(\Psi^1_t (v)\) is an odd element for all \(v \in V\). Therefore \(\Psi^1_t \in \mathcal{G} (V)\) for all \(t \geqslant 0\) and also \(\Psi^1_t\) is compatible with \(\Psi_0 \oplus B\). Since this is true for all \(t\), by approximation in the operator norm we see that \(\Psi^1 \in C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{G} (V))\) and that \(\Psi^1\) is compatible with \(\Psi_0 \oplus B\). By induction we prove then that \(\Psi^n \in C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{G} (V))\) for all \(n \geqslant 0\) and that it is compatible with \(\Psi_0 \oplus B\).

Now observe that \(\Psi_s^n (F (v))\) is a polynomial function of \((\Psi^n_s (v))_{v \in V}\) and therefore by a standard contraction argument in the Banach space \(\mathcal{A}\) we obtain that \((\Psi^n)_n\) converges, as \(n \rightarrow + \infty\), in \(C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{A})\) for some positive \(T\) which depends only on \(F\), on \(\| \Psi_0 \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)}\) and on \(\sup_{t \geqslant 0} \| B_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)}\). If we call \(\Psi\) the limit we have that \(\Psi \in C^0 ([0, T] ; \mathcal{G} (V))\) by the first part of the proof and we deduce easily that \(\Psi\) is compatible with \(\Psi_0 \oplus B\).\(\Box\)

Theorem 31. A solution to equation (23) exists for all times.

Proof. We will have global existence as soon as we can rule out explosions, that is prove that for all \(t \geqslant 0\) we have \(\| \Psi_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} < \infty\). For \(v \in V\), let \(\Theta_t (v) = \Psi_t (v) - B_t (v)\) and extend this map to an homeomorphism of \(\Lambda V\) into \(\mathcal{A}\). Observe that

\(\displaystyle \Theta_t (v) = \Theta_0 (v) + \int_0^t m_{\mathcal{A}} [(\Theta_s \otimes B_s) (\Delta F (v))] \mathrm{d} s,\)

where recall that \(m_{\mathcal{A}} : \mathcal{A} \otimes \mathcal{A} \rightarrow \mathcal{A}\) denotes the multiplication in \(\mathcal{A}\). In particular, if we consider a (linear) basis of \(\Lambda V\) denoted by \((e_A)_A\) and let \(\Theta^A_t := \Theta_t (e_A)\) and \(B^A_t := B_t (e_A)\) we have

\(\displaystyle \Theta_t^A = \Theta_0^A + \int_0^t \sum_B h_{A, B, C, D} \Theta_s^B \Theta_0^C B_s^D \mathrm{d} s,\)

for real coefficients \((h_{A, B, C, D})_{A, B, C, D}\). This is a finite-dimensional, linear system of non-autonomous ODEs in \(\mathcal{A}\) for \((\Theta_t^A)_A\), and

\(\displaystyle \sum_A \| \Theta_t^A \|_{\mathcal{A}} \leqslant \sum_A \| \Theta_0^A \|_{\mathcal{A}} + | h | \int_0^t \sum_B \| \Theta_s^B \|_{\mathcal{A}} \sum_D \| B_s^D \|_{\mathcal{A}} \sum_B \| \Theta_0^B \|_{\mathcal{A}} \mathrm{d} s,\)

with \(| h | := \sup_{A, B, C, D} | h_{A, B, C, D} |\). By Gronwall inequality

\(\displaystyle \sum_A \| \Theta_t^A \|_{\mathcal{A}} \leqslant \sum_A \| \Theta_0^A \|_{\mathcal{A}} \exp \left( | h | \int_0^t \sum_C \| B_s^C \|_{\mathcal{A}} \sum_A \| \Theta_0^A \|_{\mathcal{A}} \mathrm{d} s \right) < \infty,\)

for all \(t \geqslant 0\). In particular \(\| \Psi_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant \| \Theta_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} + \| B_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} < \infty\) for all \(t \geqslant 0\) and the solution to equation (23) exists for all times.\(\Box\)

3.3An Itô formula for solutions of SDEs

We want to prove an Itô formula for the SDE (23). For \(C \in \mathcal{L} (V, V)\), let \(Q_C \in \mathcal{L} (\Lambda V \otimes V \otimes V, \Lambda V)\) be given by

\(\displaystyle Q_C (f \otimes v \otimes w) = \langle v, C w \rangle f, \qquad f \in \Lambda V, v, w \in V. \) (29)

For \(F \in \mathcal{L} (V, \Lambda V)\) and \(G \in \Lambda V \otimes V\) we define \(G \cdot F \in \Lambda V\) by extending linearly

\(\displaystyle (f \otimes v) \cdot F = f F (v), \qquad f \in \Lambda V, v \in V,\)

where on the right hand side we have the multiplication of elements in \(\Lambda V\).

Furthermore if \(\Psi_1, \Psi_2 \in \mathcal{G} (V)\), \(K = K_1 \otimes K_2 \in \Lambda V \otimes V\) and \(F \in \mathcal{L} (V, \Lambda V)\) we write

\(\displaystyle \langle K (\Psi_1), F (\Psi_2) \rangle = K_1 (\Psi_1) F (K_2) (\Psi_2) \in \mathcal{A} . \) (30)

The definition (30) of the pairing \(\langle K (\Psi_1), F (\Psi_2) \rangle\) can be extended by linearity to any \(K \in \Lambda V \otimes V\).

Theorem 32. For the global solution to equation (23) we have

\(\displaystyle \omega (\Psi_t (G) \Psi_0 (H)) = \omega (\Psi_0 (G) \Psi_0 (H)) + \int_0^t \omega \left[ \Psi_s \left( \partial_R G \cdot F + \frac{1}{2} Q_C (\partial_R^2 G) \right) \Psi_0 (H) \right] \mathrm{d} s \) (31)

for all \(G, H \in \Lambda V\) and \(t \geqslant 0\).

Proof. We will prove the statement for \(H = 1\) and leave to the reader to generalize it to any \(H\). Fix any \(T \geqslant 0\). Note that

\(\displaystyle \| B_t - B_s \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \lesssim | t - s |^{1 / 2},\)

for all \(0 \leqslant s < t \leqslant T\). From that it follows that, if \(F\) in (23) is a polynomial of degree \(\deg (F)\) by Theorem 14, we have

\(\displaystyle \| (\Psi_t - \Psi_s) - (B_t - B_s) \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} = \left\| \int_s^t F (\Psi_s) \mathrm{d} s \right\|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \lesssim (1 + \| \Psi \|_{[0, T]})^{\deg (F)} | t - s |, \) (32)

where \(\| \Psi \|_{[0, T]} := \sup_{s \in [0, T]} \| \Psi_s \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)}\) which, by Theorem 31 and the continuity of \(\Psi\) with respect to the time \(t\), is finite for every \(T \in \mathbb{R}_+\). By Taylor formula (see Lemma 10, Theorem 12 and Theorem 14) applied to polynomial \(G\) of degree \(\deg (G)\) we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} \| G (\Psi_r) - G (\Psi_s) - m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_s \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s)) \partial_R G + \\ \left. \left. + \frac{1}{2} m_{\mathcal{A}}^2 (\Psi_s \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s) \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s)) (\partial_R \partial_R G) \right\| \right._{\mathcal{A}} \lesssim (1 + \| \Psi \|_{[0, T]})^{\deg (F) (\deg (G) - 2)} | r - s |^{3 / 2} \end{array} \) (33)

since \(\| \Psi_t - \Psi_s \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \lesssim (1 + \| \Psi \|_{[0, T]})^{\deg (F)} | t - s |^{1 / 2}\) by (32). In a similar way it is possible to obtain

\(\displaystyle \| \langle \partial_R G (\Psi_u), F (\Psi_u) \rangle - \langle \partial_R G (\Psi_s), F (\Psi_u) \rangle \|_{\mathcal{A}} \lesssim (1 + \| \Psi \|_{[0, T]})^{\deg (F) (\deg (G) - 2)} | u - s |^{1 / 2} \) (34)

where \(\langle \partial_R G (\Psi_s), F (\Psi_u) \rangle\) is defined by linear extension of the relation (30). On the other hand, by (32), we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_s \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s)) (\partial_R G) & = & \int_s^r \langle \partial_R G (\Psi_s), F (\Psi_u) \rangle \mathrm{d} u\\ & & + m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_s \otimes (B_r - B_s)) (\partial_R G)\\ & = & \int_s^r \langle \partial_R G (\Psi_u), F (\Psi_u) \rangle \mathrm{d} u + \langle \partial_R G (\Psi_s), (B_r - B_s) \rangle\\ & & + O_{\mathcal{A}} ((1 + \| \Psi \|_{[0, T]})^{\deg (F) (\deg (G) - 2)} | r - s |^{3 / 2}) \end{array} \) (35)

In a similar way, using inequality (32) we obtain

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} m_{\mathcal{A}}^2 (\Psi_s \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s) \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s)) (\partial_R^2 G) = m_{\mathcal{A}}^2 (\Psi_s \otimes (B_r - B_s) \otimes (B_r - B_s)) (\partial_R^2 G)\\ + O_{\mathcal{A}} ((1 + \| \Psi \|_{[0, T]})^{\deg (F) (\deg (G) - 2)} | r - s |^{3 / 2}) . \end{array}\) (36)

Furthermore, using the fact that \(\partial_R G (\Psi_s)\) is independent of \((B_r - B_s)\) and \(((B_r - B_s) \otimes (B_r - B_s))\) is independent of \(\partial_R^2 G (\Psi_s)\) (this is due to the fact that \(\Psi_s\) is a function of \(\{ \Psi_0, B_u | u \in [0, s] \}\) and, moreover, \(B\) is independent of \(\Psi_0\) and has independent increments, we have:

\(\displaystyle \omega (\langle \partial_R G (\Psi_s), (B_r - B_s) \rangle) = \langle \omega ((B_r - B_s)), \omega (\partial_R G (\Psi_s)) \rangle = 0\)

where on the r.h.s. we understand that averages w.r.t. the state \(\omega\) are taken component-wise, and

\(\displaystyle \omega [\langle \partial_R^2 G (\Psi_s), (B_r - B_s) \otimes (B_r - B_s) \rangle_{V \otimes V}]\)
\(\displaystyle = \langle \omega [(\partial_R^2 G (\Psi_s))], \omega [((B_r - B_s) \otimes (B_r - B_s))] \rangle\)
\(\displaystyle = (r - s) \omega [Q_C (\partial_R^2 G (\Psi_s))] .\)

By taking a partition \(\{ t_i \}_{i \in \{ 0, \ldots, n \}}\), of diameter \(\rho_n \rightarrow 0\) as \(n \rightarrow + \infty\), of \([0, t]\) and exploiting inequality (33) we obtain

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \omega (G (\Psi_t)) & = & \omega (G (\Psi_0)) + \sum_{i = 0}^{n + 1} \omega (G (\Psi_{t_i}) - G (\Psi_{t_{i - 1}}))\\ & = & \omega (G (\Psi_0)) + \sum_{i = 1}^{n + 1} \omega (m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_{t_{i - 1}} \otimes (\Psi_{t_i} - \Psi_{t_{i - 1}})) \partial_R G)\\ & & + \frac{1}{2} \omega (m_{\mathcal{A}}^2 (\Psi_s \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s) \otimes (\Psi_r - \Psi_s)) (\partial_R^2 G)) + O (\rho_n^{1 / 2})\\ & = & \omega (G (\Psi_0)) + \omega \left( \int_0^t \langle \partial_R G (\Psi_u), F (\Psi_u) \rangle \mathrm{d} u \right)\\ & & + \frac{1}{2} \sum_{i = 1}^{n + 1} (t_i - t_{i - 1}) \omega [Q_C (\partial_R^2 G (\Psi_{t_i}))] + O (\rho_n^{1 / 2}), \end{array}\)

where the constants in \(O\) are proportional to \((1 + \| \Psi \|_{[0, T]})^{\deg (F) \deg (G)}\) and do not depend on the partition. Taking the limit \(n \rightarrow + \infty\), and so \(\rho_n \rightarrow 0\), and using the fact that \(\omega [Q_C (\partial_R^2 G (\Psi_s))]\) is continuous in \(s\), (\(G (\Psi_s)\) being a continuous function from \(\mathbb{R}_+\) to \(\mathcal{A}\) since \(\Psi \in C^0 ([0, T], \mathcal{G} (V))\)) we obtain the thesis.\(\Box\)

3.4Invariant measures

Definition 33. We say that a continuous linear functional \(\rho : \mathcal{A} \rightarrow \mathbb{C}\) is an invariant measure for the equation (23) if for any \(G \in \Lambda V\) and any \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+\) we have

\(\displaystyle \rho (G (\Psi_t)) = \rho (G (\Psi_0)) . \) (37)

There is a simple condition, analogous to the Fokker–Planck equation for commutative SDEs, for checking the invariance of the functional \(\rho\).

Lemma 34. If \(\Psi\) is a solution to (23) and \(\rho : \mathcal{A} \rightarrow \mathbb{C}\) a continuous linear functional for which

\(\displaystyle \rho (\mathcal{L}G (\Psi_0)) = 0, \qquad G \in \Lambda V ,\) (38)

where

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}G := \partial_R G \cdot F + \frac{1}{2} Q_C (\partial_R^2 G),\)

then \(\rho\) is an invariant measure for eq. (23) in the sense of Definition 33. In other words, eq. (38) is a sufficient condition for \(\rho\) to be an invariant measure.

Proof. Let \((v_A)_A\) be a (finite) linear basis of \(\Lambda V\) and observe that we have \(\mathcal{L} v_A = \sum_B \kappa_A^B v_B\) for a suitable family \((\kappa_A^B)_{A, B}\) of constants. Let \(\mathfrak{P}_A (t) := \rho (v_A (\Psi_t))\), by Itô formula (31) we have that \(t \in \mathbb{R}_+ \mapsto (\mathfrak{P}_A (t))_A\) is the unique solution of the system of ODEs

\(\displaystyle \partial_t \mathfrak{P}_A (t)\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \partial_t \rho (v_A (\Psi_t)) = \rho ((\mathcal{L} v_A) (\Psi_t))\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \sum_B \kappa_A^B \rho (v_B (\Psi_t)) = \sum_B \kappa^B_A \mathfrak{P}_B (t), \) (39)

with initial condition \(\mathfrak{P}_A (0) = \rho (v_A (\Psi_0))\). On the other hand condition (38) implies that

\(\displaystyle \sum_B \kappa^B_A \mathfrak{P}_B (0) = \sum_B \kappa^B_A \rho (v_B (\Psi_0)) = \rho (\mathcal{L}v_A (\Psi_0)) = 0,\)

which means that \((t \mapsto \mathfrak{P}_A (0))_A\) is also a solution of (38), and therefore by uniqueness of solutions to this system of ODEs we conclude that \(\mathfrak{P}_A (t) = \mathfrak{P}_A (0)\) for all \(t \geqslant 0\). Since the expectations of the form (37) are linear combinations of \((\mathfrak{P}_A (t))_A\) the proof is complete.\(\Box\)

The following theorem gives a sufficient condition on the SDE (23) to have the invariant measure (42).

Theorem 35. For any \(U \in \Lambda_{\operatorname{even}} V\), consider the SDE

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t (v) = \Psi_0 (v) + \int_0^t \Psi_s (A v - \langle C \partial_R U, v \rangle) \mathrm{d} s + B_t (v) \qquad t \geqslant 0, v \in V, \) (40)

where, under the state \(\omega\), \(B\) is a Brownian motion with correlation \(C : V \rightarrow V\) and \(\Psi_0\) a Gaussian initial condition with correlation \(C_A\) (defined in equation (27)) independent of \(B\). Assume that the condition

\(\displaystyle A^{\mathrm{T}} C_A - C_A A = 0 \) (41)

holds. Then linear functional \(\tilde{\omega} : \mathcal{A} \rightarrow \mathbb{C}\) given by

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\omega} (\cdot) := \omega (\cdot e^{- 2 U (\Psi_0)}), \) (42)

is an invariant measure for (40).

Proof. The proof is a consequence of Lemma 34. Denote \(\omega^{\Psi_0} (G) := \omega (G (\Psi_0))\) the law of \(\Psi_0\), and observe that for \(H \in \Lambda V \otimes V\) we have the integration by parts formula

\(\displaystyle \omega (m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_0 \otimes \Psi_0) (H)) = \omega (\Psi_0 (Q_{C_A} (\partial_R H))),\)

where \(Q_C\) is defined in (29). Then for all \(G \in \Lambda V\) we have

\(\displaystyle \omega [m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_0 \otimes \Psi_0) ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A) \partial_R G e^{- 2 U})] = \omega^{\Psi_0} [Q_{C_A} (\partial_R ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A) \partial_R G e^{- 2 U}))]\)
\(\displaystyle = \omega^{\Psi_0} (Q_{C_A} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A \otimes \mathbb{I}) \partial_R^2 G) e^{- 2 U}) - 2 \omega^{\Psi_0} (Q_{C_A} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A \otimes \mathbb{I}) (\partial_R G \otimes_2 \partial_R U) ) e^{- 2 U})\)

where \(\otimes_2\) is defined as \((f \otimes v) \otimes_2 (g \otimes w) = (f g) \otimes v \otimes w\) for \(f, g \in \Lambda V\) and \(v, w \in V\). Observe that using the trace \(\operatorname{Tr}\) on \(\Lambda V \otimes V \otimes V\) given by \(\operatorname{Tr} (f \otimes v \otimes w) = f \langle v, w \rangle\), the antisymmetry of \(\partial_R^2 G \in \Lambda V \otimes V \otimes V\) in the last two factors and equality (28) we have

\(\displaystyle Q_{C_A} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A \otimes \mathbb{I}) \partial_R^2 G) =\operatorname{Tr} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A \otimes C_A) \partial_R^2 G)\)
\(\displaystyle =\operatorname{Tr} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes - C_A A \otimes \mathbb{I}) \partial_R^2 G) = \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{Tr} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes \mathbb{I} \otimes (A^{\mathrm{T}} C_A + C_A A)) \partial_R^2 G)\)
\(\displaystyle = - \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{Tr} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes \mathbb{I} \otimes C) \partial_R^2 G) = - \frac{1}{2} Q_C (\partial_R^2 G) .\)

Moreover, from (41) we have also

\(\displaystyle Q_{C_A} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A \otimes \mathbb{I}) (\partial_R G \otimes_2 \partial_R U) ) =\operatorname{Tr} ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A \otimes C_A) (\partial_R G \otimes_2 \partial_R U)) =\operatorname{Tr} (\partial_R G \otimes_2 (A^{\mathrm{T}} C_A \partial_R U))\)
\(\displaystyle =\operatorname{Tr} \left( \partial_R G \otimes_2 \left( \frac{A^{\mathrm{T}} C_A + C_A A}{2} \partial_R U \right) \right) +\operatorname{Tr} \left( \partial_R G \otimes_2 \left( \frac{A^{\mathrm{T}} C_A - C_A A}{2} \partial_R U \right) \right)\)
\(\displaystyle = - \frac{1}{2} \operatorname{Tr} (\partial_R G \otimes_2 (C \partial_R U)) = - \frac{1}{2} \langle \partial_R G, C \partial_R U \rangle,\)

since we assumed that \(A^{\mathrm{T}} C_A - C_A A = 0\) and used (28) again. Therefore

\(\displaystyle \omega (m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_0 \otimes \Psi_0) ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A) (\partial_R G) e^{- 2 U})) = - \frac{1}{2} \omega^{\Psi_0} (Q_C (\partial_R^2 G) e^{- 2 U}) + \omega^{\Psi_0} (\langle \partial_R G, C \partial_R U \rangle e^{- 2 U}) .\)

Now observing that

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}G (\Psi_0) = m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_0 \otimes \Psi_0) ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A) (\partial_R G)) + \Psi_0 \left( - \langle \partial_R G, C \partial_R U \rangle + \frac{1}{2} Q_C (\partial_R^2 G) \right)\)

we deduce that

\(\displaystyle \omega (\mathcal{L}G (\Psi_0) e^{- 2 U (\Psi_0)}) = \omega (m_{\mathcal{A}} (\Psi_0 \otimes \Psi_0) ((\mathbb{I} \otimes A) (\partial_R G)) e^{- 2 U (\Psi_0)}) +\)

\(\displaystyle + \omega \left[ \Psi_0 \left( - \langle \partial_R G, C \partial_R U \rangle + \frac{1}{2} Q_C (\partial_R^2 G) \right) e^{- 2 U (\Psi_0)} \right] = 0.\)
\(\Box\)

3.5Long-time behavior for small non-linearity

We will now investigate the existence of solutions which are globally bounded in time. In the commutative setting, non-linear equations can have globally bounded solutions only if the non-linearity stays uniformly small or if it shows some coercivity. Like all notions of positivity, also coercivity however does not apply well in the Grassmann setting. The only kind of coercive term we have identified is a linear drift with a negative sign. This is a very mild coercive term, but it turns out to be enough provided the non-linearity is small enough. Consider the equation

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t (v) = \Psi_0 (v) + \int_0^t (\Psi_s (A v) + \lambda \Psi_s (F (v))) \mathrm{d} s + B_t (v), \qquad t \geqslant 0, v \in V, \) (43)

for \(\Psi \in C ([0, T] ; \operatorname{Hom} (V, \mathcal{A}))\), with \(\lambda \geqslant 0\) and \(F \in \operatorname{Hom} (V, \Lambda V)\) where (as in Section 3.1) \(A\) is an operator with eigenvalues having strictly negative real part less then \(- \lambda_A < 0\). In this setting we introduce the notion of stationary solution to equation (43), extending the one we defined for the linear case in Section 3.1.

Definition 36. We say that \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{G} (V))\) is a stationary solution to equation (43) (extended to all \(t \in \mathbb{R}\)) of norm at most \(K \in \mathbb{R}_+\) if the following two conditions hold:

  1. \(\sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| \Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant K\),

  2. \((\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)_{t \in \mathbb{R}}\) solves the following integral equation

    \(\displaystyle \Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t = \lambda \int_{- \infty}^t \Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\tau} (F (e^{A \tau} v)) \mathrm{d} \tau + B^A_t \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}\) (44)

    where \(B^A_t (v) = \Xi (e^{A (t - \cdot)} (v))\) is the OU motion introduced in Section 3.1.

Hereafter we write

\(\displaystyle L = L (A, C) = \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| B^A_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} < \infty,\)

and we denote by \(\mathfrak{K}_K \subset C^0 (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{G} (V))\) the set of \(\Phi \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{G} (V))\) such that

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| \Phi_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant K ,\)

for some constant \(K > 0\).

Theorem 37. Assume \(K \geqslant 4 L (A, C)\) and suppose that all the eigenvalues of \(A\) have negative real part that are less or equal than \(- \lambda_A < 0\), then there exists \(\lambda_0 = \lambda_0 (K, L, F, \lambda_A) > 0\) depending on \(K\), \(L\) and \(F\) such that if \(| \lambda | \leqslant 2 \lambda_0\) there exists a unique stationary solution of norm at most \(K\) to equation (43).

Proof. It is a simple application of the Banach fixed-point theorem by noting that the map

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{K}_{\lambda} (\Psi)_t := \lambda \int_{- \infty}^t \Psi_{\tau} (F (e^{A (t - \tau)} v)) \mathrm{d} \tau + B^A_t, \qquad t \in \mathbb{R},\)

is a contraction in \(\mathfrak{K}_K\) for \(\lambda\) small. Indeed, note that \(B^A \in \mathfrak{K}_K\) and if \(\Psi \in \mathfrak{K}_K\) we have, by Theorem 14,

\(\displaystyle \| \mathcal{K}_{\lambda} (\Psi)_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant | \lambda | \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} (1 + K)^{\deg (F)} \int_{- \infty}^t e^{- \lambda_A (t - \tau)} \mathrm{d} \tau + L (A, C),\)

where \(\| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V}\) is defined by (15). Therefore \(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}\) maps \(\mathfrak{K}_K\) into itself provided \(| \lambda | \leqslant 2 \lambda_0 := \lambda_0 (K, L, F, \lambda_A)\) with \(\lambda_0\) such that

\(\displaystyle 2 \lambda_0 \frac{\| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} (1 + K)^{\deg (F)}}{\lambda_A} + 2 L \leqslant K. \) (45)

Furthermore

\(\displaystyle \| \mathcal{K}_{\lambda} (\Psi_1)_t - \mathcal{K}_{\lambda} (\Psi_2)_t \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant \frac{| \lambda |}{\lambda_A} (\deg (F)) \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} (1 + K)^{\deg (F) - 1} \| \Psi_{1, t} - \Psi_{2, t} \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} .\)

Therefore, if we take \(\lambda_0\) small enough to satisfy also

\(\displaystyle \frac{2 \lambda_0}{\lambda_A} \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} (\deg (F)) (1 + K)^{\deg (F) - 1} < 1 \) (46)

the map \(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}\) is a strict contraction.\(\Box\)

Remark 38. The function \(\lambda_0\) in Theorem 37 can be taken to be a decreasing function of the parameters \(L\) and \(K\).

We introduce an approximation for the solution to equation (44). Let \(X \in \mathcal{G} (V)\) independent of \(B^A_t\) and consider the element \(\Psi_{- T, t}^X \in C^0 (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{G} (V))\) which is \(\Psi_{- T, t}^X = X\) if \(t \leqslant - T\), for some \(T > 0\), and is a solution to the SDE

\(\displaystyle \Psi_{- T, t}^X = X (e^{A (t + T)} v) + \lambda \int_{- T}^t F (\Psi_{- T, t}^X (e^{A (t - \tau)} v)) + B^A_t (e^{A (t + T)} v) - B^A_{- T} (e^{A (t + T)} v),\)

when \(t > - T\) and extended as \(\Psi_{- T, t}^X = X\) for \(t \leqslant - T\).

Lemma 39. Under the assumptions of Theorem 37, take \(\| X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant K / 8\), then, for any \(| \lambda | \leqslant \lambda_0\), we have that \(\Psi_{- T, \cdot}^X \in \mathfrak{K}_K\). Moreover \(\Psi_{- T, \cdot}^X\) converges to \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\cdot}\) in \(\mathcal{G} (V)\) as \(T \rightarrow + \infty\), uniformly on compact subsets of \(\mathbb{R}\).

Proof. Let \(\tau \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(R_{\tau} := \sup_{t \in (- \infty, \tau]} \| \Psi_{- T, t}^X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)}\). Theorem 14 gives the bound

\begin{eqnarray*} R_{\tau} & \leqslant & \| X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} + \| B_{- T}^A \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} +\\ & & + \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} \frac{\lambda_0}{\lambda_A} (1 + R_{\tau})^{\deg (F)} + L (A, C)\\ & \leqslant & \frac{3 K}{8} + \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} \frac{\lambda_0}{\lambda_A} (1 + R_{\tau})^{\deg (F)} + L (A, C) \end{eqnarray*}

where we used that \(\| B_{- T}^A \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant L \leqslant K / 4\) and \(\| X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant K / 8\). On the other hand by definition of \(\lambda_0\), we have

\(\displaystyle \frac{3 K}{8} + \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} \frac{\lambda_0}{\lambda_A} (1 + K)^{\deg (F)} + L (A, C) \leqslant \frac{7}{8} K < K\)

for any \(\tau > 0\). Consider the set \(Z = \{ \tau \in \mathbb{R}: R_{\tau} \leqslant K \}\). The set \(Z\) is non-empty, since \(R_{\tau} \leqslant \frac{1}{2} K\) when \(\tau \leqslant - T\), closed since \(\tau \mapsto R_{\tau}\) is continuous and open since \(\tau \mapsto R_{\tau}\) is an increasing continuous function of \(\tau\) and if \(\tau \in Z\) then \(R_{\tau + \varepsilon} < K\) for \(\varepsilon > 0\) small enough so \((\tau - \varepsilon, \tau + \varepsilon) \in Z\). Then we must have \(Z =\mathbb{R}\) and

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| \Psi_{- T, t}^X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant K,\)

for \(| \lambda | \leqslant \lambda_0\). For the convergence we argue as follows. If \(t \geqslant - T\), by Theorem 14, we have that

\begin{eqnarray*} \| \Psi_{- T, t}^X - \Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}} \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} & \lesssim & e^{- \lambda_A (t + T)} (\| X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} + \| B_{- T}^A \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)}) +\\ & & + | \lambda | \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} (1 + K)^{\deg (F)} \int_{- \infty}^{- T} e^{- \lambda_A (t - \tau)} \mathrm{d} \tau\\ & & + \alpha \int_{- T}^t e^{- \lambda_A (t - \tau)} \| \Psi_{- T, \tau}^X - \Psi_{\tau}^{\mathfrak{s}} \| \mathrm{d} \tau . \end{eqnarray*}

where \(\alpha = | \lambda | \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} (\deg (F)) (1 + K)^{\deg (F) - 1}\). By Gronwall inequality, we obtain

\begin{eqnarray*} \| \Psi_{- T, t}^X - \Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}} \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} & \leqslant & \left( \| X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} + \frac{K}{3} + \frac{| \lambda | \| F \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} V} (1 + K)^{\deg (F)}}{\lambda_A} \right) \times\\ & & \times \left( e^{- \lambda_A (t + T) + \alpha t} \int_{- T}^t e^{- \alpha \tau} d \tau \right)\\ & \lesssim & e^{- \lambda_A (t + T)} e^{\alpha t} (e^{\alpha T} - e^{- \alpha t}) \lesssim e^{- (\lambda_A - \alpha) (t + T)} . \end{eqnarray*}

Since \(\lambda_A - \alpha > 0\), by the condition (46), we have that

\(\displaystyle \| \Psi_{- T, t}^X - \Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}} \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \lesssim e^{- (\lambda_A - \alpha) T},\)

uniformly in \(T\) when \(t > P > - T\) for any such fixed \(P \in \mathbb{R}\). This proves that \(\Psi_{- T, t}^X\) converges to \(\Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}}\) uniformly on compact sets.\(\Box\)

We are now in position to prove the main result of this section, the stochastic quantization of a Gibbs measure on Grassmann Gaussian variables.

Theorem 40. Assume that under the state \(\omega\) the random variable \(X\) is a Grassmann Gaussian with covariance \(C_A\) and \(B^A\) an OU motion independent of \(X\). Assume that \(K\) is a constant such that \(L \leqslant K / 3\) and \(\| X \|_{\mathcal{G} (V)} \leqslant K / 8\). Let \(U \in \Lambda V_{\operatorname{even}}\) and let

\(\displaystyle F (v) = - \frac{\lambda}{2} \langle C \partial_R U, v \rangle\)

for \(v \in V\). Then \(\tilde{\omega} (\cdot) = \omega (\cdot e^{- \lambda U (X)})\) is an invariant measure for the equation equation (43) and for all \(| \lambda | \leqslant \lambda_0 (K, L, F, \lambda_A)\), for any \(t \in \mathbb{R}\) and for any \(G \in \Lambda V\), we have that

\(\displaystyle \omega (G (X) e^{- \lambda U (X)}) = \omega (G (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)) \omega (e^{- \lambda U (X)}) .\)

Proof. Note that \((\Psi_{- T, t}^X)_{t \geqslant - T}\) is a solution of the SDE (40) starting at \(- T\) with the value \(X\) and with potential \(\lambda U / 2\). Therefore by Theorem 35 we have that, for any \(t \geqslant - T\),

\(\displaystyle \omega (G (X) e^{- \lambda U (X)}) = \omega (G (\Psi_{- T, - T}^X) e^{- \lambda U (X)}) = \omega (G (\Psi_{- T, t}^X) e^{- \lambda U (X)}) .\)

By Lemma 39 we can take the limit at the right hand side as \(T \rightarrow + \infty\), obtaining

\(\displaystyle \omega (G (X) e^{- U (X)}) = \omega (G (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t) e^{- U (X)}) .\)

Since (by construction) \(X\) is independent of \(B^A_t\) under \(\omega\) (and therefore under \(\tilde{\omega}\)) and since \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t\) is a function only of \(B^A\) we get

\(\displaystyle \omega (G (X) e^{- U (X)}) = \omega (G (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)) \omega (e^{- U (X)}) .\)
\(\Box\)

Remark 41. Note that by taking \(G = \lambda U\) we obtain

\(\displaystyle 1 = \omega (e^{\lambda U (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)}) \omega (e^{- \lambda U (X)})\)

which implies in particular that both \(\omega (e^{\lambda U (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)})\) and \(\omega (e^{- \lambda U (X)})\) are nonzero and that

\(\displaystyle \omega (e^{- \lambda U (X)}) = [\omega (e^{\lambda U (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)})]^{- 1}\)

\(\displaystyle \frac{\omega (G (X) e^{- \lambda U (X)})}{\omega (e^{- \lambda U (X)})} = \omega (G (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)) = \omega (e^{\lambda U (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t)}) \omega (G (X) e^{- \lambda U (X)}) .\)

Remark 42. Let now \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}, \sigma}\) be the stationary solution corresponding to the potential \(\sigma U\) for \(\sigma \in [0, \lambda]\). Then

\(\displaystyle \dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} \sigma} \log \omega (e^{- \sigma U (X)}) = - \frac{\omega (U (X) e^{- \sigma U (X)})}{\omega (e^{- \sigma U (X)})} = - \omega (U (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s, \sigma}}_t))\)

and integrating \(\sigma\) gives a remarkable formula for the logarithm of the partition function \(\omega (e^{- \lambda U (X)})\) in terms of the parametric stochastic quantization \((\Psi^{\mathfrak{s, \sigma}}_t)_{\sigma \in [0, 1]}\):

\(\displaystyle - \log \omega (e^{- \lambda U (X)}) = \int_0^{\lambda} \omega (U (\Psi^{\mathfrak{s, \sigma}}_t)) \mathrm{d} \sigma .\)

4Infinite dimensional SDEs

We want to study SDEs of the form

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} \Psi_t = (A \Psi_t + \lambda F (\Psi_t)) \mathrm{d} t + \mathrm{d} B_t \) (47)

in the case where \(\Psi\) takes values in \(\mathcal{G} (V)\) with \(V =\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n,\) or \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{C}^n\), or \(C^{\infty} (\mathbb{T}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) for some \(n \in \mathbb{N}_0\) and with \(A\) a (possibly unbounded) linear operator on \(V\). Heuristically, \(d\) represents the dimension of base space and \(n\) is the number of Grassmannian fields considered. Here, we discuss in detail the case \(V = \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\), but the considerations in this section can be generalized to the other cases, or more generally, to any nuclear space of smooth functions (see Remark 43).

The main difference between the finite and infinite dimensional case for Grassmann SDEs is that in general there is no natural topology on \(\mathcal{G} (V)\). The space \(V = \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) is a Fréchet nuclear space. Since not every element of \(\mathcal{G} (V)\) is continuous with respect to this topology, we consider here the space \(\mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V) \subset \mathcal{G} (V)\), the subset of \(\mathcal{G} (V)\) for which \(X \in \mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) if and only if \(X\) restricted to \(V \subset \Lambda V\) is a continuous linear map from \(V\) to \(\mathcal{A}\). Hereafter we always consider \(X \in \mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) both as an homomorphism from the algebra \(\Lambda V\) into \(\mathcal{A}\), and as a continuous linear map from \(V\) into \(\mathcal{A}\).

Since \(V\) is a Fréchet nuclear space whose dual is nuclear, the set of continuous linear maps from \(V\) into \(\mathcal{A}\) can be identified with the tensor product \(V^{\mathord{*}} \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A} = (\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A}\), see, e.g., Proposition 50.5 in [174]. Here \(V^{\mathord{*}}\) is the topological dual of \(V\), the Schwartz space of tempered distribution \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) and the symbol \(\hat{\otimes}\) in the product \(V^{\ast} \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A}\) means that we take the closure of \(V^{\ast} \otimes \mathcal{A}\) with respect to any natural topology on \(V^{\ast} \otimes \mathcal{A}\) being all of them equivalent since \(V^{\ast}\) is nuclear (see, e.g., Theorem 50.1 in [174]). In other words we can look upon \(\mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) as the subset of elements of \((\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \otimes \mathcal{A}\) that can be extended to a homomorphism from \(\Lambda V\) into \(\mathcal{A}\).

Remark 43. The identification of the set of continuous linear maps from \(V\) into \(\mathcal{A}\) with \(V^{\ast} \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A}\) does not hold if \(V\) is a generic topological vector space, for example if \(V\) is a Banach space. Indeed in the case where \(V\) is a infinite dimensional Banach space the space \(V^{\ast} \otimes \mathcal{A}\) has not unique natural norm. In particular the completion \(V^{\ast} \otimes_{\varepsilon} \mathcal{A}\) with respect the injective norm (which is the weaker of them) we have \(V^{\ast} \otimes_{\varepsilon} \mathcal{A} \subsetneq \mathcal{L} (V, \mathcal{A})\) (see, e.g., [148]). For this reason we prefer to work in the case where \(\mathcal{L} (V, \mathcal{A}) = V^{\ast} \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A}\) which is for example when \(V\) is a Fréchet nuclear space.

Using a reasoning similar to the one exploited in Lemma 7, it is possible to prove that \(\mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) is a closed subset of \(V^{\mathord{*}} \otimes \mathcal{A}\) with respect to the strong topology (of both \(V^{\mathord{*}} = \mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) and \(\mathcal{A}\)), i.e. if \((X_{\nu})_{\nu}\) is a net in \(\mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) converging to \(X\) in the topology of \(V^{\mathord{*}} \otimes \mathcal{A}\), then \(X\) can be extended to an homomorphism from \(\Lambda V\) into \(\mathcal{A}\). Working on \(\mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) is not so easy since \((\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \otimes \mathcal{A}\) is not even a Fréchet space. For this reason we introduce now some subsets of \(\mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) (which are analogous to Besov spaces) which admit a stronger metric topology.

Remark 44. In what fallow we use the notation if \(X \in \mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) we write, for any \(k \in \{ 1, \ldots, n \}\), the map \(X^k \in \mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^n))\) as follows

\(\displaystyle X^k (f) = X (f \otimes e_k)\)

where \(\{ e_k \}_{k = 1, \ldots, n}\) is the standard orthonormal basis of \(\mathbb{R}^n\).

We recall here only the definition of the Besov norm for functions taking values in a Banach space, further references and details are given in Appendix B (see also [9, 10]). Let \(K_i \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d)\), with \(i \geqslant - 1\), be the functions corresponding to the Littlewood–Paley blocks used in the definition of Besov spaces \(B^s_{p, q} := B^s_{p, q} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathbb{R})\), where \(s \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(p, q \in [1, + \infty]\). If \(X \in \mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) we define

\(\displaystyle \Delta_i X (x) = (\Delta_i X^1 (x), \ldots, \Delta_i X^k (x)) := (X (K_i (x - \cdot) \otimes e_k))_{k = 1, \ldots, n}, \qquad x \in \mathbb{R}^d, i \geqslant - 1\)

where \(\{ e_k \}_{k = 1, \ldots n}\) is the standard basis of \(\mathbb{R}^n\). The function \(\Delta_i X^k : \mathbb{R}^d \rightarrow \mathcal{A}\) can be identified with an element \(\Delta_i X^k \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \hat{\otimes} \mathcal{A} = \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})\) and we define

\(\displaystyle \| X \|_{B^s_{p, q, n} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})} := \sum_{k = 1}^n \left[ \sum_{i \geqslant - 1} 2^{q i s} \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \| \Delta_i X^k (x) \|_{\mathcal{A}}^p \mathrm{d} x \right)^{q / p} \right]^{1 / q} .\)

Given \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), we say that \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\) when \(X \in \mathcal{G}^{- \infty} (V)\) and \(\| X \|_{B^s_{\infty, \infty} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})} < + \infty\). Thus we introduce the natural metric \(d_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)} (X, Y) := \| X - Y \|_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)} := \| X - Y \|_{B^s_{\infty, \infty, n} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})}\). Hereafter we always use the notation \(\mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A}) = B^s_{\infty, \infty, n} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})\) and \(\mathcal{C}^s = \mathcal{C}^s_1 (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathbb{R})\). Moreover \(\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}}\) will denote the topological dual of \(\mathcal{C}^s\).

Lemma 45. The set \(\mathcal{G}^s (V)\) with the metric \(d_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)}\) is a complete metric space.

Proof. The proof is analogous to that of Lemma 7.\(\Box\)

If \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\) we can extend \(X\) to a linear map \(\mathcal{L} ((\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n), \mathcal{A})\), i.e. to a linear map from the space \(\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) (where \(\mathcal{C}^{s \ast} = \mathcal{C}^{s \ast} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathbb{R})\) is the (strong topological) dual of \(\mathcal{C}^s\) and we identify \(\mathbb{R}^n\) with its dual using its standard basis) taking values into the algebra \(\mathcal{A}\). This extension can be obtained in the following way: If \(g \in \mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\), for any sequence \((g_{\ell})_{\ell} \subset \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) converging to \(g\) with respect to the \(\text{weak}^{\ast}\) topology of \(\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) we have that \((X (g_{\ell}))_{\ell}\) converges in \(\mathcal{A}\) to a unique element

\(\displaystyle X (g) := \lim_{n \rightarrow + \infty} X (g_n) .\)

The map \(X\) now defined on all of \(\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) is continuous. In other words, if \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\) then it can be identified with an element of \(\mathcal{L} ((\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n), \mathcal{A})\). Furthermore it is simple to prove, using suitable \(\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) approximations, that, if \(g, h \in \mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\), then we have

\(\displaystyle X (g) X (h) = - X (h) X (g) . \) (48)

In the case \(s > 0\), for any \(x \in \mathbb{R}^d\) the Dirac delta distribution \(\delta_x\) is in \(\mathcal{C}^{s \ast}\), this implies that we can identify the linear maps \(X^k\) with some function in \(\mathcal{C}^s (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})\) defined as, using an abuse of notation,

\(\displaystyle X^k (x) := X (\delta_x \otimes e_k) .\)

Using this identification we have that for any \(f_n \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d)\), \(X (f \otimes e_k) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} X^k (x) f (x) \mathrm{d} x\), where the integral is taken in Bochner sense.

The relation (48) has important consequences. For any positive integer \(\ell \in \mathbb{N}_0\), let

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{cc} \Lambda^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \subset & \underbrace{(\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \otimes_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \otimes_{\pi} \cdots \otimes_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)}\\ & \text{$\ell$ times} \end{array}\)

be the closure of \(\Lambda^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) in \(\otimes^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) using the natural inclusion \(i_{\Lambda^{\ell} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)}\) of \(\Lambda^{\ell} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) in \(\otimes^{\ell} (\mathcal{C}^{s \ast} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) (see the discussion after Lemma 10 for more details). Here \(\otimes_{\pi}\) is the projective tensor product of Banach spaces whose norm is defined as follows: If \(f \in \otimes^{\ell} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) then

\(\displaystyle \| f \|_{\otimes^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)} := \inf \left\{ \sum_{i = 1}^k \prod_{j = 1}^{\ell} \| f^i_j \|_{(\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)}, \operatorname{where}f = \sum_{i = 1}^k f^i_1 \otimes \cdots \otimes f^i_{\ell} \right\} .\)

Lemma 46. If \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\), then, for every \(\ell \in \mathbb{N}_0\), it can be extended to a continuous homomorphism from \(\bigoplus_{i = 1}^{\ell} \Lambda_{\pi}^i (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) into \(\mathcal{A}\). Here \(\bigoplus_{i = 1}^{\ell} \Lambda_{\pi}^i (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) is considered as equipped with the product obtained from the standard product of \(\bigoplus_{i = 1}^{\infty} \Lambda_{\pi}^i (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) via the projection onto \(\bigoplus_{i = 1}^{\ell} \Lambda_{\pi}^i (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\).

Proof. The proof is based on the fact that \(X\) can be identified with an element of \(\mathcal{L} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}}, \mathcal{A})\) and on the relation (48).\(\Box\)

We want now to define a natural notion of function \(F\) of an element \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\). For example, if \(s > 0\), and writing \(Y = F (X)\), we want to be able to take \(F = P_{\ell}\) of the form

\(\displaystyle Y^j (x) := Y (\delta_x \otimes e_j) =\)
\(\displaystyle = \sum_{k_1, \ldots, k_{\ell} = 1}^n \sum_{(h_1, \ldots, h_{\ell}) \in D} K^j_{(h_1, \ldots, h_{\ell}), k_1, \ldots, k_{\ell}} X^{k_1} (x + h_1) X^{k_2} (x + h_2) \cdots X^{k_{\ell}} (x + h_{\ell}) ,\) (49)

where \(D \subset \mathbb{R}^{d \ell}\) is some finite set and \(K_{(h_1, \ldots, h_{\ell}), k_1, \ldots, k_{\ell}}^j \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(j \in \{ 1, \ldots, k \}\) (\(\ell \in \mathbb{N}_0\) in this case represent the degree of the homogeneous polynomial \(P_{\ell}\)). When \(\ell\) is odd, the function \(P_{\ell}\) defined above maps \(P_{\ell} (\mathcal{G}^s (V)) \subset \mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})\) and furthermore for any \(g, h \in \mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}}\) and \(k, p \in \{ 1, \ldots, n \}\) we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} Y^k (g) Y^p (h) = - Y^p (h) Y^k (g)\\ Y^k (g) X^p (h) = - X^p (h) Y^k (g) . \end{array} \) (50)

We want to consider some more general setting than the described in equation (49) that maintains the important property (50).

Consider \(P_{\ell} \in \mathcal{L} (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n, \Lambda^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n))\), then \(P_{\ell}\) can be seen as an element of \((\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \otimes \Lambda^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\). Using the same technique as before we can introduce the Banach space \(\mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \Lambda^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)) \subset (\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \otimes \Lambda^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) equipped with its natural norm. In this case we will use the notation

\(\displaystyle P_{\ell} (X) := X \circ P_{\ell}, \) (51)

where \((X \circ P_{\ell}) (v) = X (P_{\ell} (v)) \in \mathcal{A}\) for any \(v \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\). Consider \(u \in \mathbb{N}_0\), if \(F \in \mathcal{C}^s_n \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \right)\) there are some \(P_{\ell} \in \mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \Lambda^{\ell}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n))\) (for odd \(\ell \in \mathbb{N}\)) such that

\(\displaystyle F := \sum_{j = 0}^u P_{2 j + 1} .\)

Thus we define \(F (X) = \sum_{j = 0}^u P_{2 j + 1} (X) .\) The function \(F\) defined above can be interpreted as an odd polynomial of degree \(2 u + 1\) in the Grassmannian random variable \(X\).

Definition 47. We say that the linear function \(F \in \mathcal{C}^s_n \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \right)\) is a standard odd function of degree \(2 u + 1\) if there is a sequence of functions \(F_r \in \mathcal{C}^s_n \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^k \Lambda^{2 j + 1} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \right)\) (where \(\Lambda^{2 j + 1} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) = \underbrace{(\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \otimes \cdots \otimes (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)}_{\text{$2 j + 1$ times}}\) and \(\otimes\) is the algebraic tensor product) such that for any \(g \in V\) we have \(F_r (g) \rightarrow F (g)\), as \(r \rightarrow + \infty\), in \(\bigoplus_{j = 0}^k \Lambda^{2 j + 1}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) equipped with its standard topology.

Remark 48. When \(s > 0\), an example of a function \(F = (F^1, \ldots, F^n)\) satisfying Definition 47 is

\(\displaystyle F^k (X) (x) := F (X) (\delta_x \otimes e_k) = X^k (x) \sum_{k_1, k_2 = 1}^n \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \mathcal{G}_{k_1, k_2} (x - y) X^{k_1} (y) X^{k_2} (y) \mathrm{d} y,\)

where \(\mathcal{G}_{k_1, k_2} \in \mathcal{C}^{s + \epsilon}\) are functions that decrease faster than any polynomial at infinity. We note that a finite dimensional approximation of \(F^k (X)\) is given by

\(\displaystyle F^k_r (X) (x) = \sum_{k_1, k_2 = 1}^n \sum_{D_i \in \mathcal{P}_r} X^k (x) X^{k_1} (x - x_i) X^{k_2} (x - x_j) \int_{D_i} \mathcal{G}_{k_1, k_2} (y) \mathrm{d} y,\)

where \((\mathcal{P}_r)_{r \in \mathbb{N}}\) is sequence of increasing partitions of \(\mathbb{R}^d\) for which

\(\displaystyle \max_{k_1, k_2 = 1, \ldots, n} \sup_{D_i \in \mathcal{P}_r} \left| \int_{D_i} \mathcal{G}_{k_1, k_2} (y) \mathrm{d} y \right| \rightarrow 0\)

as \(r \rightarrow \infty\).

Theorem 49. Let \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), let \(F \in \mathcal{C}^s_n \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \right)\) be a standard odd function of degree \(2 u + 1\) (see Definition 47), and consider \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d))\). Then \(F (X) \in \mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})\), furthermore for any \(v_1, v_2 \in \mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) we have

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} F (X) (v_1) F (X) (v_2) = - F (X) (v_2) F (X) (v_1)\\ F (X) (v_1) X (v_2) = - X (v_2) F (X) (v_1) . \end{array} \) (52)

Proof. The fact that \(F (X) \in \mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})\) follows from Lemma 46. The relations (52) are obvious when \(F \in \mathcal{C}^s_n \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^k \Lambda^{2 j + 1} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \right)\). Using a finite dimensional approximation \(F_r\) converging to \(F\), required by Definition 47, the relations (52) are extended to the more general case.\(\Box\)

Remark 50. An important consequence of Theorem 49 is that if \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\) and \(F\) satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem 49, \(F (X)\) can be extended to a Grassmann random variable (which in the following we denote by the same symbol \(F (X)\)) in \(\mathcal{G}^s (V)\). Furthermore, \(F (X)\) and \(X\) are compatible (in the sense of the corresponding definition in Section 2).

We are now in the position to give a precise meaning to equation (47) and to the various hypotheses on its data. Let the operator \(A\) be the (generally unbounded) generator of a strongly continuous and exponentially contractive semigroup \((e^{A t})_{t \geqslant 0}\) on \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)\). Moreover consider the stationary Ornstein-Uhlenbeck motion

\(\displaystyle B^A_t (v) = \Xi (\mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} \otimes e^{A (t - \cdot)} v), \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}, v \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n, \) (53)

where \(\Xi\) is a Grassmann Gaussian noise on \(\mathcal{G} (C^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}_+) \otimes \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n)\) with covariance \(\mathbb{I} \otimes C\) (we consider on \(C^{\infty}_c (\mathbb{R}_+) \otimes \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) the pre-Hilbert space structure given by the natural inclusion of \(C^{\infty}_c (\mathbb{R}_+) \otimes \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\) in \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathbb{R}^d) \otimes \mathbb{R}^n\)).

Definition 51. We say that the process \(\Psi \in C^0 ([0, T], \mathcal{G}^s (V))\) satisfies equation (47) with initial condition \(\Psi_0\) if \(F\) is a standard odd function of finite degree, \(\Psi_0 \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\) is independent of \(\Xi\), and for any \(t \in [0, T]\) and any \(v \in V\) we have

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t (v) = \Psi_0 (e^{A t} v) + \lambda \int_0^t F (\Psi_s) (e^{A (t - s)} v) \mathrm{d} s + B^A_t (v) - B_0^A (e^{A t} v) ,\) (54)

where the integral in the variable \(s\) is taken in Bochner sense with respect the norm of \(\mathcal{A}\).

Hereafter, we introduce the following notations if \(F \in \mathcal{C}^s_n \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \right)\):

\(\displaystyle \| F \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n, \pi} := \sup_{k = 1, \ldots, n} \| F^k \|_{\mathcal{C}^s \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1}_{\pi} \mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \right)},\)
\(\displaystyle \deg (F) := 2 u + 1.\)

Theorem 52. Suppose that \(F \in \mathcal{C}^s_n \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1}_{\pi} (\mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \otimes \mathbb{R}^n) \right)\) and that \(F\) satisfies Definition 47, then for any \(X, Y \in \mathcal{G}^s (V)\) compatible we have

\(\displaystyle \| F (X) \|_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)} \leqslant \| F \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n, \pi} (1 + \| X \|_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)})^{\deg (F)} \) (55)
\(\displaystyle \| F (X) - F (Y) \|_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)}\) \(\displaystyle \leqslant\) \(\displaystyle \deg (F) \| F \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n, \pi} \| X - Y \|_{\mathcal{G}^{s (V)}} \times\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \times (1 + \max (\| X \|_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)}, \| Y \|_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)}))^{\deg (F) - 1} .\) (56)

Proof. Let \(F^k_r \in \mathcal{C}^s \left( \mathbb{R}^d, \bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1} \mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}} \right)\) (where \(\bigoplus_{j = 0}^u \Lambda^{2 j + 1} \mathcal{C}^{s \mathord{*}}\) is the usual algebraic antisymmetric tensor product) be the approximation of \(F^k\) required in Definition 47, then using the same reasoning of Theorem 12 and Theorem 14, if \(K_i\) is the kernel corresponding to the Littlewood–Paley block \(\Delta_i\), we obtain

\(\displaystyle \sup_{k = 1, \ldots, n} \| F^k_r (X) (K_i (x - \cdot)) \|_{\mathcal{A}} \leqslant \sup_{k = 1, \ldots, n} \| F^k_r (K_i (x - \cdot)) \|_{\Lambda_{\pi} \mathcal{C}^{s \ast}} (1 + \| X \|_{\mathcal{G}^s (V)})^{\deg (F)} . \) (57)

If we multiply both sides of (57) by \(2^{i s}\), take the limit as \(r \rightarrow + \infty\) and afterwords the supremum on \(x\) and \(j\) we get inequality (55). In a similar way we get (56) by generalizing the proof of Theorem 12 and Theorem 14.\(\Box\)

Remark 53. Hereafter we use the notations, for \(x \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle f_F (x) = \| F \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n, \pi} (1 + x)^{\deg (F)},\) (58)
\(\displaystyle g_F (x) = \deg (F) \| F \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n, \pi} (1 + x)^{\deg (F) - 1} .\) (59)

We introduce some conditions on \(A\) and \(\Xi\) in order to prove a result on existence and uniqueness of solutions to (54).

Definition 54. We say that the unbounded operator \(A\) on \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^d)\), and the Grassmann Gaussian noise \(\Xi\) with covariance \(C\) are adapted to the space \(\mathcal{G}^s (V)\), for a given \(s \in \mathbb{R}\), if

  1. The operator \(A\) generates a contraction semigroup on \(\mathcal{C}^s (\mathbb{R}^d)\) such that \(\| e^{A t} \|_{\mathcal{L} (\mathcal{C}^s (\mathbb{R}^d), \mathcal{C}^s (\mathbb{R}^d))} \lesssim e^{- \lambda_A t}\), \(t \geqslant 0\), for some strictly positive constant \(\lambda_A > 0\).

  2. The Gaussian process \(B^A\) defined by (53) belongs to \(C (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{G}^s (V))\) and furthermore we have \(\sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| B_t^A \|_{\mathcal{C}^s (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})} < + \infty\).

Theorem 55. Suppose that \(F\) satisfies Definition 47 and \(A, \Xi\) satisfy Definition 54 and let \(\Psi_0\) be a variable in \(\mathcal{G}^s (V)\) independent of the Grassmann Gaussian noise \(\Xi\). Then there exists a \(T > 0\) such that there is a unique solution \(\Psi \in C ([0, T], \mathcal{G}^s (V))\) to equation (54). Furthermore \(\Psi_t\) is compatible with \((\Psi_0, \Xi, \Psi_s)\) for every \(t, s \in [0, T]\).

Proof. The proof is a straightforward generalization of that of Theorem 30 for the case of our infinite dimensional setting.\(\Box\)

In order to obtain solutions for all times it is not possible to extend directly Theorem 31, since its proof was essentially based on the hypothesis that \(V\) is finite dimensional. On the other hand the proofs of Theorem 37 and Lemma 39 do not depend on the dimension. For this reason we introduce the space \(\mathfrak{K}_K \subset C (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{G}^s (V))\) defined as the set of functions \(\Psi_t \in C (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{G}^s (V))\) such that

\(\displaystyle \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| \Psi_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})} \leqslant K,\)

for some constant \(K > 0\), the function \(L\) given by

\(\displaystyle L (A, C) = \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| B^A_t \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})},\)

and the equation for stationary solutions corresponding to (54) as

\(\displaystyle \Psi_t^{\mathfrak{s}} = \lambda \int_{- \infty}^t F (\Psi_{\tau}^{\mathfrak{s}} (e^{A (t - \tau)})) \mathrm{d} \tau + B^A_t \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}.\) (60)

We also denote by \(\Psi_{- T, t}^X\) the Grassmann process defined by \(\Psi_{- T, t}^X = X\) if \(t \leqslant - T\), \(T \geqslant 0\), and such that \(\Psi_{- T, t}^X\) is the solution to equation (54) with initial condition \(X\) at time \(t = - T\).

Theorem 56. Suppose that \(F\) satisfies Definition 47 and \(A, \Xi\) satisfy Definition 54 and let \(K \geqslant 4 L (A, C)\). Then there exists \(\lambda_0 = \lambda_0 (K, L, F, \lambda_A) > 0\) such that for any \(| \lambda | \leqslant 2 \lambda_0\) there exists a unique (stationary) solution to equation (60) in \(\mathfrak{K}_K\). Furthermore for any \(| \lambda | \leqslant \lambda_0\) and for any random variable \(X \in \mathcal{G}^s_{\mathcal{}} (V)\), independent of \(B^A_t\) and such that \(\| X \|_{\mathcal{C}^s_n (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A})} < K / 8\), we have that \(\Psi^X_{- T, t}\) converges, as \(T \rightarrow + \infty\), in \(\mathcal{G}^s (V)\) to \(\Psi^{\mathfrak{s}}_t\) uniformly on compact subsets of \(\mathbb{R}\).

Proof. Due to our appropriate infinite dimensional setting the proof is a straightforward generalization of Theorem 37 and Lemma 39.\(\Box\)

Remark 57. The quantity \(\lambda_0\) defined in Theorem 56 has to satisfy the following inequalities

\(\displaystyle \frac{2 \lambda_0 f_F (K)}{\lambda_A} + 2 L \leqslant K, \qquad \frac{2 \lambda_0}{\lambda_A} g_F (K) < 1, \) (61)

where \(f_F\), \(g_F\) and \(\lambda_A\) are the quantities introduced in Remark 53 and Definition 54 respectively.

5The Yukawa\(_2\) model

We are now in position to study the stochastic quantization for an Euclidean fermionic QFT. We consider, as an example, the Euclidean counterpart of the Yukawa model describing the interaction of a scalar field with a spin \(1 / 2\) field in a \(1 + 1\) Minkowski space-time (“Yukawa\(_2\) model”) [130, 45, 21, 113, 172]. We will not attempt here to remove the UV cutoff and therefore the dimension of the space will not play any fundamental role. The Yukawa\(_2\) model contains already the main features of a large class of Euclidean fermionic theories. It will be clear from the considerations in this section that similar results for other fermionic models are easily derivable as long as we keep an ultraviolet cut-off and we limit ourselves to a perturbative and massive regime.

5.1Definition of the model

We define the Euclidean Yukawa\(_2\) quantum field theory following Osterwalder and Schrader [131]. The Euclidean free Fermions, introduced by Osterwalder and Schrader (compare with [131], page 282 and equation (3.3), page 284), are given by a quadruplet \(\tilde{\chi} = (\chi, \bar{\chi}) \in \mathcal{G} (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4)\) of complex Grassmann Gaussian fields. Here we employ the convention of denoting by \(\chi, \bar{\chi} \in \mathcal{G} (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^2)\) two independent fields (in particular the bar over \(\bar{\chi}\) does not denote complex conjugation). By considering two fields \(\chi, \bar{\chi}\) we are, “doubling the dimension”. This “dimension doubling”, also employed by Osterwalder and Schrader, has the purpose of obtaining an Euclidean covariance with the right symmetry properties.

We adapt to the present case some notation for complex Grassmann Gaussian random variables which we introduced in Section 2.3. We consider \(V =\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4 \subseteq L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4\) as a complex pre-Hilbert space with an Hermitian scalar product denoted \((\cdot, \cdot)_V\) equipped with the standard real structure \(c\) given by the complex conjugation (i.e. \(c f = \bar{f}\)) determining the bilinear form \(\langle f, g \rangle_c := (c f, g)_V\). Given an operator \(A\) on \(V\), we denote by \(A^c\) the complex-conjugated Hilbert adjoint, i.e. \(A^c = c A^{\ast} c\), \(A^{\ast}\) being the Hilbert adjoint of \(A\). Finally, we say that an operator \(K\) is \(c\)-antisymmetric when \(K^c = - K\). (recall Definition 19 and Definition 20).

With these notations, the fields \(\tilde{\chi}\) are defined to be complex Grassmann Gaussian variables with covariance

\(\displaystyle \omega (\tilde{\chi} (f) \tilde{\chi} (g)) = \langle f, K g \rangle_c, \qquad f, g \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4 . \) (62)

where \(K : L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4 \rightarrow L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4\) is given by the following \(c\)-antisymmetric bounded operator

\(\displaystyle K = (m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1} C_f, \qquad C_f := \left( \begin{array}{cc} 0 & \left( \not{\nabla} + m_f \right)\\ - \left( \not{\nabla} + m_f \right)^c & 0 \end{array} \right) . \) (63)

Here \(\Delta\) is the Laplacian on \(\mathbb{R}^2\), \(m_f > 0\) is the fermion mass and \(\not{\nabla}\) is the Euclidean Dirac operator in \(2\) Euclidean space-time dimensions. Explicitly, \(\not{\nabla} := \gamma_1 \nabla_1 + \gamma_2 \nabla_2\) with \(\nabla_i\) the partial derivatives with respect to \(x_i\), \(i = 1, 2\), and with \(\gamma_1, \gamma_2 : \mathbb{C}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb{C}^2\) the Euclidean Dirac matrices, i.e. Hermitian matrices satisfying

\(\displaystyle \gamma_a \gamma_b + \gamma_b \gamma_a = 2 \delta_{a b}, \qquad a, b = 1, 2. \) (64)

Concretely, we can take \(\gamma_1, \gamma_2\) to be simply the first two Pauli matrices:

\(\displaystyle \gamma_1 = \left( \begin{array}{cc} 0 & 1\\ 1 & 0 \end{array} \right), \quad \gamma_2 = \left( \begin{array}{cc} 0 & - \mathrm{i}\\ \mathrm{i} & 0 \end{array} \right) .\)

Note that \(K = C_f^{- 1}\) and that

\(\displaystyle \tilde{C}_f = (m_f^2 - \Delta)^{1 / 2} K = (m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 2} C_f\)

is also bounded and antisymmetric in \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4\), hence the covariance matrix of the field \(\tilde{\psi}\) can also be written as

\(\displaystyle \omega (\tilde{\chi} (f) \tilde{\chi} (g)) = \langle (m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 4} f, \tilde{C}_f (m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 4} g \rangle_c .\)

Therefore we can realize \(\tilde{\chi}\) as

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\chi} (f) := \tilde{\zeta} ((m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 4} f), \qquad f \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4,\)

where \(\tilde{\zeta} \in \mathcal{G} (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4)\) is the complex Grassmann Gaussian field specified by the bounded covariance

\(\displaystyle \omega (\tilde{\zeta} (f) \tilde{\zeta} (g)) = \langle f, \tilde{C}_f g \rangle_c, \qquad f, g \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4 .\)

As a consequence, in this specific realization, we have

\(\displaystyle \| \tilde{\chi} (f) \| = \| \tilde{\zeta} ((m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 4} f) \| \lesssim \| (m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 4} f \|_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4} < \infty, \) (65)

where the implicit constant in the inequality depends only on \(m_f\). This realization gives better spatial regularity properties to the Euclidean fields \(\tilde{\chi}\).

In order to describe the Yukawa\(_2\) model we need also to introduce an Euclidean boson field \(\varphi\) of mass \(m_b > 0\): it is the centered Gaussian field \(\varphi\) on \(\mathbb{R}^2\) with covariance \((m^2_b - \Delta)^{- 1}\) where \(m_b > 0\) is looked upon as a fixed parameter and \(\Delta\) is the Laplacian on \(\mathbb{R}^2\). We can realize \(\varphi\) as the canonical multiplication operator on the Hilbert space \(\mathcal{H}_{\varphi} = L^2 (\mathcal{C}^{- \kappa}_{\text{loc}} (\mathbb{R}^2), \nu)\) where \(\nu\) is the centered Gaussian measure on \(\mathcal{C}^{- \kappa}_{\text{loc}} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) (the local version of the \(\mathcal{C}^{- \kappa}\) Besov space) with covariance \((m^2_b - \Delta)^{- 1}\) and \(\kappa > 0\) is any small positive quantity. We shall denote by \(\omega_{\varphi}\) the state associated with \(\nu\) and \(\mathcal{C}^{- \kappa}_{\text{loc}} (\mathbb{R}^2)\).

In addition, large scale and small scale cutoffs are introduced as follows. Fix a smooth positive functions \(h \in C^{\infty} (\mathbb{R}^2)\) with compact support and let \(\mathfrak{a}\) be a regular enough (see below for the precise regularity) continuous function with compact support such that \(\mathfrak{a} (0) = 1\). Take \(\varepsilon > 0\) and let \(\mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon} (x) = \mathfrak{a} (x / \varepsilon) \varepsilon^{- 2}\). Then we define new (regularized) Grassmann variables

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\psi} (x) := \tilde{\chi} (\mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon} (\cdot - x)), \qquad x \in \mathbb{R}^2 .\)

Then \(\tilde{\psi} \in \mathcal{G}^s (\mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4)\) for some \(s > 0\) (in general depending on the regularity of \(\mathfrak{a}\) see below).

Following the argument of Section 6 of [131] the Euclidean Yukawa\(_2\) model is defined as follows.

Definition 58. The approximate fermionic Schwinger functions \((\rho^n_{\varepsilon, h})_n\) of the Yukawa\(_2\) model on \(\mathbb{R}^2\) with infrared (IR) cutoff \(h\) and ultraviolet (UV) fermion cutoff \(\varepsilon\) are given by the Euclidean averages

\(\displaystyle \rho^n_{\varepsilon, h} (f_1, \ldots, f_n) = \frac{\omega \otimes \omega_{\varphi} (\tilde{\psi} (f_1) \cdots \tilde{\psi} (f_n) e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})})}{\omega \otimes \omega_{\varphi} (e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})})} \) (66)

with potential \(V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})\) given by the unbounded (non-self-adjoint) operator on \(\mathcal{H} \otimes \mathcal{H}_{\varphi}\), where \(\mathcal{H}\) is the fermionic Hilbert space,

\(\displaystyle V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi}) = \lambda \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \mathrm{d} x h (x) (\bar{\psi} (x) \cdot \psi (x)) \varphi (x), \) (67)

\(\lambda \in \mathbb{R}\) is a coupling constant and \(\omega\), resp. \(\omega_{\varphi}\) are the fermion, resp. boson, states described above.

Note that there is no difficulty in making sense of (66), due to the following observations (see also [131]). The map \(x \in \mathbb{R}^2 \mapsto \tilde{\psi}_{\varepsilon} (x) \in \mathcal{A}= \mathcal{L} (\mathcal{H})\) is \(\mathcal{C}^s\) due to the presence of the regularization \(\varepsilon > 0\) and the regularity of the Euclidean field \(\tilde{\psi}\) given in (65). The massive Gaussian free field \(\varphi\) has (local) regularity \(\mathcal{C}^{- \kappa}_{\operatorname{loc}}\) for arbitrarily small \(\kappa > 0\), therefore, taking \(s > \kappa\), by a standard Besov estimates for Banach valued functions (see e.g. [9, 10]), we have, for any fixed \(\varphi \in \mathcal{C}^{- \kappa}_{\operatorname{loc}} (\mathbb{R}^2)\)

\(\displaystyle \| V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi}) \|_{\mathcal{A}} \lesssim \lambda \| \tilde{\psi} \|^2_{\mathcal{C}^{\kappa} (\mathcal{A})} \| h \varphi \|_{B^{- \kappa}_{1, 1} (\mathbb{R}^2)} \lesssim \lambda \| h \varphi \|_{\mathcal{C}^{- \kappa} (\mathbb{R}^2)} .\)

It is also not difficult to see that the map \(V_h : \mathcal{C}^{- \kappa} \rightarrow \mathcal{A}\) given by \(V_h : \varphi \mapsto V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})\) is linear and continuous in \(\varphi\), therefore measurable and that

\(\displaystyle \omega \otimes \omega_{\varphi} (\tilde{\psi} (f_1) \cdots \tilde{\psi} (f_n) e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}) = \omega (\tilde{\psi} (f_1) \cdots \tilde{\psi} (f_n) \omega_{\varphi} (e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}))\)

e.g. by proving this first for a piecewise approximation of \(\varphi\) and then taking the limit. Moreover we have (by the properties of the Bochner integral)

\(\displaystyle \| \omega_{\varphi} (e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}) \|_{\mathcal{A}} \leqslant \omega_{\varphi} (\exp (C | \lambda | \| h \varphi \|_{\mathcal{C}^{- \kappa}})) < \infty,\)

for all \(\lambda \in \mathbb{R}\). We conclude that the averages in (66) are well defined since

\(\displaystyle | (\omega \otimes \omega_{\varphi}) (\tilde{\psi} (f_1) \cdots \tilde{\psi} (f_n) e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}) | \leqslant \| \tilde{\psi} (f_1) \cdots \tilde{\psi} (f_n) \omega_{\varphi} (e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}) \|_{\mathcal{A}} < \infty .\)

The strict positivity \(Z = \omega \otimes \omega_{\varphi} (e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}) > 0\) has been proved in [131] via the Feynman–Kac formula and reflection positivity for cutoffs \(h\) which are symmetric with respect to. reflection in one spatial coordinate. In [163] the positivity of \(Z\) is proved in Lemma 4.1 there using the Matthew–Salam formula. Alternatively, provided \(\lambda\) is small enough one can use Remark 41 to assert that \(Z \neq 0\).

By expanding the quantity \(\omega_{\varphi} (e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}) \in \mathcal{A}\) in power series and integrating away the Gaussian field we obtain

\(\displaystyle \omega_{\varphi} (e^{- V_h (\varphi, \tilde{\psi})}) = e^{\mathcal{V}_h (\tilde{\psi})},\)

with a purely fermionic potential given by

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{V}_h (\tilde{\psi}) = \lambda^2 \int_{(\mathbb{R}^2)^2} (h \bar{\psi} \cdot \psi) (x) \mathcal{G} (x - y) (h \bar{\psi} \cdot \psi) (y) \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} y. \) (68)

Here \(\mathcal{G} = (m^2_b - \Delta)^{- 1}\) is the Green's function of the operator \((m^2_b - \Delta)\) and, as before, \(m_b\) is the boson mass. This gives us the purely fermionic representation of the Schwinger functions (66) given by:

\(\displaystyle \rho^n_{\varepsilon, h} (f_1, \ldots, f_n) := \omega_{r Y}^h (\tilde{\psi} (f_1) \cdots \tilde{\psi} (f_n)) = \frac{\omega (\tilde{\psi} (f_1) \cdots \tilde{\psi} (f_n) e^{\mathcal{V}_h (\tilde{\psi})})}{\omega (e^{\mathcal{V}_h (\tilde{\psi})})} . \) (69)

Perturbation theory suggests that the boson field requires a mass renormalization \(\mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon}\) which depends on the fermion cutoff \(\varepsilon > 0\). In theory one would need to modify the bosonic Gaussian field covariance to include a mass renormalization and let \(\varphi\) have covariance \((m^2_b - \Delta + a_{\varepsilon})^{- 1}\) instead of the original \((m^2_b - \Delta)^{- 1}\). However in this paper we will not discuss the removal of the UV cutoff and keep \(\varepsilon > 0\) fixed. Therefore, for simplicity, we ignore this mass renormalization.

5.2Stochastic quantization

In order to stochastically quantize the Yukawa model, we consider the Grassmannian differential equation

\(\displaystyle \mathrm{d} \tilde{\Psi}_t^h = ((\Delta - m_f^2) \tilde{\Psi}_t^h + \lambda^2 F_{h, r Y} (\tilde{\Psi}_t^h)) \mathrm{d} t + \mathrm{d} \tilde{B}_{\varepsilon, t}, \quad \tilde{\Psi}_t^h := (\Psi^h_t, \bar{\Psi}^h_t) : \mathbb{R}^2 \rightarrow \mathcal{A} \otimes \mathbb{C}^4 \) (70)

where \(\Delta\) is the Laplacian on \(\mathbb{R}^2\), the function \(F_{h, r Y}\) is defined as

\(\displaystyle F_{h, r Y} (\tilde{\Psi}_t^h) = C_f \mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathord{*} 2} \ast \left[ \frac{h}{2} \left( \int_{\mathbb{R}^2} \mathcal{G} (y - \cdot) h (y) \Psi_t^h (y) \bar{\Psi}_t^h (y) \mathrm{d} y \right) J \tilde{\Psi}_t^h \right],\)

with \(\mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon} (x) = \varepsilon^{- 2} \mathfrak{a} \left( \frac{x}{\varepsilon} \right)\), \(\varepsilon > 0\), \(\mathfrak{a} : \mathbb{R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^2\) is regular enough function with compact support (see below for the more precise requirements), \(\mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathord{*} 2} = \mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon} \mathord{*} \mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon}\), \(h : \mathbb{R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) is a smooth function (in the beginning with compact support, later on we will consider \(h \equiv 1\)) and \(\mathcal{G}\) is the Green's function of the elliptic operator \((m_b^2 - \Delta)\) and \(J : \mathbb{C}^4 \rightarrow \mathbb{C}^4\) is given by

\(\displaystyle J = \left( \begin{array}{cc} 0 & 1\\ - 1 & 0 \end{array} \right) .\)

Finally, \(\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon, t}\) is a Brownian motion given by

\(\displaystyle \tilde{B}_{\varepsilon, t} (v) = \tilde{\Xi} (\mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} \otimes (\mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon} \ast v)), \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}, v \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4,\)

where \(\tilde{\Xi}\) is a white noise on the pre-Hilbert space \(L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+) \otimes \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4 \subseteq L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+) \otimes B_{2, 2}^{- 1 / 2} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4\) equipped with the scalar product

\(\displaystyle ((f, g)) = (f, (m_f^2 - \Delta)^{- 1 / 2} g)_{L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+ \times \mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4} \qquad f, g \in L^2 (\mathbb{R}_+) \otimes \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4,\) (71)

and having the bounded covariance \(\tilde{C}_f = (m^2_f - \Delta)^{- 1 / 2} C_f\).

We claim that the stationary solution to the Grassmann SPDEs (70) is associated to the Yukawa measure \(\omega^h_{r Y} (\cdot) = \omega (\cdot e^{\mathcal{V}_h})\). In particular it is the solution to the integral equation

\(\displaystyle \tilde{\Psi}^h_t (v) = \lambda^2 \int_{- \infty}^t F_{h, r Y} (\tilde{\Psi}_{\tau}^h) (e^{A (t - \tau)} v) \mathrm{d} \tau + \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t} (v), \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}, v \in \mathbb{C}^4,\) (72)

where \(A = \Delta - m_f^2\) and the regularized noise \(\tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}\) is given by the expression

\(\displaystyle \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t} (v) = \tilde{\Xi} (\mathbb{I}_{(- \infty, t]} \otimes e^{A (t - \cdot)} (\mathfrak{a}_{\varepsilon} \ast v)), \qquad t \in \mathbb{R}, v \in \mathcal{S} (\mathbb{R}^2) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4, \) (73)

where \(\tilde{\Xi}\) is the white noise introduced above.

Remark 59. From now on we will make the following assumption. We fix \(\delta > 0\) and \(\gamma > 0\), let \(\alpha = \gamma + 1 / 2 + 2 \delta\) and take \(\mathfrak{a} \in B^{\alpha}_{1, \infty} (\mathbb{R}^2) \cap \mathcal{C}^{\alpha} (\mathbb{R}^2)\). Again we recall that \(\varepsilon > 0\) will be kept fixed.

The results which we will prove is the following:

Theorem 60. With the above notation and conditions on \(F_{h, r Y}\) consider \(\alpha, \gamma, \delta\) and \(\mathfrak{a}\) as in Remark 59. Then for \(0 \leqslant | \lambda | < \lambda_{\varepsilon, h}\) small enough equation (70) admits a unique stationary solution \(\tilde{\Psi}^h\) in \(\mathcal{C}^{\gamma} (\mathbb{R}^d, \mathcal{A}) \otimes \mathbb{C}^4\) given by (72).
