Suppose furthermore that each \(Z_n (\lambda)\) is real analytic and that there is a real analytic function \(0 \trianglelefteqslant K : U \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) such that \(Z_n \trianglelefteqslant K\), then \(Z (\lambda)\) is real analytic in a neighborhood of \(0\) and \(Z \trianglelefteq K\).

Proof. Consider \(\ell^{\ast} \in X^{\ast}\) such that \(\| \ell^{\ast} \|_{X^{\ast}} \leqslant 1\) and define \(\ell^{\ast}_{Z_n} (\lambda) := \ell^{\ast} (Z_n (\lambda))\). Since \(Z_n\) are real analytic also \(\ell^{\ast}_{Z_n}\) is real analytic, and also \(\ell^{\ast}_{Z_n} (\lambda) \rightarrow \ell^{\ast}_Z (\lambda) := \ell^{\ast} (Z (\lambda))\) for any \(\lambda \in U\). Furthermore \(\ell^{\ast}_{Z_n} \trianglelefteqslant S_{Z_n} \trianglelefteqslant K\) which implies that, by Remark 88, for any \(R\) such that \(K (R)\) is well defined, we have

\(\displaystyle \sup_{\lambda \in \mathbb{C}, | \lambda | \leqslant R} | \ell^{\ast}_{Z_n} (\lambda) | \leqslant K (R),\)

i.e. \(\ell^{\ast}_{Z_n}\) are uniformly bounded in a (complex)-neighborhood of \(0\). This means by Vitali-Porter theorem (see, i.e. [154] Chapter 2, Section 2.4) that also \(\ell^{\ast}_Z (\lambda)\) is real analytic for \(\lambda \in U \cap \{ | \lambda | < R \}\). Since \(\ell^{\ast}_Z\) is real analytic for any \(\ell^{\ast} {\in X^{\ast}}^{}\) such that \(\| \ell^{\ast} \|_{X^{\ast}} = 1\), the function \(\tilde{\ell} (Z (\lambda))\) is real analytic in \(U \cap \{ | \lambda | < R \}\) which implies, by Theorem 93, that \(Z\) is real analytic in \(U \cap \{ | \lambda | < R \}\) which is a non-empty neighborhood of \(0\).

Finally we have

\(\displaystyle \left| \frac{\mathrm{d}^k S_Z}{\mathrm{d} \lambda^k} (0) \right| = \sup_{\ell^{\ast} \in X^{\ast}, \| \ell^{\ast} \|_{X^{\ast}} = 1} \left| \frac{\mathrm{d}^k \ell^{\ast}_Z}{\mathrm{d} \lambda^k} (0) \right| \leqslant \sup_{\ell^{\ast} \in X^{\ast}, \| \ell^{\ast} \|_{X^{\ast}} = 1, n \in \mathbb{N}} \left| \frac{\mathrm{d}^k \ell^{\ast}_{Z_n}}{\mathrm{d} \lambda^k} (0) \right| \leqslant K^{(k)}\)

where we used that, from the Vitali-Porter theorem, \(\frac{\mathrm{d}^k \ell^{\ast}_{Z_n}}{\mathrm{d} \lambda^k}\) converges to \(\frac{\mathrm{d}^k \ell^{\ast}_Z}{\mathrm{d} \lambda^k}\) uniformly.\(\Box\)

Remark 95. It is easy to generalize the proof of Theorem 94 replacing the convergence of \(Z_n (\lambda)\) to \(Z (\lambda) \in X\) in the norm of \(X\), by the convergence of \(Z_n (\lambda)\) to \(Z (\lambda) \in X\) with respect to a (weaker) norm of a Banach space \(X' \supset X\) in which \(X\) is densely contained.

Let \(P : X \rightarrow X\) be a polynomial map, i.e. there are some \(P_0, P_1, \ldots, P_n\) such that \(P_k : X^k \rightarrow X\) are continuous \(k\)-linear symmetric maps such that

\(\displaystyle P (x) := \sum_{k = 0}^n P_k (x, \ldots, x), \quad x \in X.\)

We also suppose that there is \(\mathfrak{P} : \mathbb{R} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\) defined as

\(\displaystyle \mathfrak{P} (\lambda) := \sum_{k = 0}^n \| P_k \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^k, X)} \lambda^k, \quad \text{for} \lambda \in \mathbb{C},\)

where \(\| \cdot \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^k, X)}\) is the natural operator norm of \(\mathcal{L} (X^k, X)\) that is the space of \(k\)-linear maps between \(X^k\) into \(X\). Suppose that \(Z_{P, C} : U \rightarrow X\) (where \(U \subset \mathbb{R}\) is a neighborhood of 0) is the unique solution to the equation

\(\displaystyle Z_{P, C} (\lambda) = \lambda P (Z (\lambda)) + C \) (102)

where \(\lambda \in U\) and \(C \in X\). We are now ready to prove a general a-priori estimate for equations of the form (102).

Theorem 96. Suppose that a solution \(Z_{P, C}\) to equation (102) is differentiable an infinite number of time in \(\lambda = 0\), then we have \(Z_{P, C} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P, C}\) where \(K_{P, C}\) is the unique analytic solution (in a neighborhood of \(0\)) to the equation

\(\displaystyle K_{P, C} (\lambda) = \lambda \mathfrak{P} (K_{P, C} (\lambda)) + \| C \|_X . \) (103)

Proof. If we take the derivatives in the variable \(\lambda\) in the point \(\lambda = 0\) and write

\(\displaystyle Z_{P, C}^{(n)} := \frac{1}{n!} \frac{\mathrm{d}^n Z_{P, C}}{\mathrm{d} \lambda^n} (0) \in X,\)

we obtain from the formal series (100) and equation (102)

\(\displaystyle Z^{(0)}_{P, C} = C, \quad Z^{(1)}_{P, C} = P (Z^{(0)}) = P (C)\)

and, for \(r \geqslant 2\),

\(\displaystyle Z^{(r)}_{P, C} = \sum_{k = 0}^n \sum_{j_1 + \cdots + j_k = r - 1} P_k (Z^{(j_1)}_{P, C}, \ldots, Z^{(j_k)}_{P, C}) .\)

This implies that

\(\displaystyle \| Z^{(0)}_{P, C} \|_X = \| C \|_X, \quad \| Z^{(1)}_{P, C} \| \leqslant \mathfrak{P} (\| C \|_X)\)

and, for \(r \geqslant 2\),

\(\displaystyle \| Z^{(r)}_{P, C} \| \leqslant \sum_{k = 0}^n \sum_{j_1 + \cdots + j_{2 k + 1} = r - 1} \| P_{2 k + 1} \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^{2 k + 1}, X)} \| Z^{(j_1)}_{P, C} \|_X \cdots \| Z^{(j_{2 k + 1})}_{P, C} \|_X . \) (104)
\(\displaystyle \)

If we set \(S_{Z_{P, C}} = \sum_{n = 0}^{+ \infty} \| Z^{(n)}_{P, C} \| \lambda^n\), then we have

\begin{eqnarray*} S_{Z_{P, C}} (\lambda) & = & \| C \|_X + \sum_{r = 1}^{+ \infty} \| Z^{(k)}_{P, C} \| \lambda^r\\ & \trianglelefteqslant & \| C \|_X + \sum_{r = 1}^{+ \infty} \lambda^r \sum_{k = 0}^n \sum_{j_1 + \cdots + j_k = r - 1} \| P_k \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^k, X)} \| Z^{(j_1)}_{P, C} \|_X \cdots \| Z^{(j_k)}_{P, C} \|_X\\ & \trianglelefteqslant & \| C \|_X + \lambda \sum_{k = 0}^n \sum_{r = 0}^{+ \infty} \lambda^r \sum_{j_1 + \cdots + j_k = r} \| P_k \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^k, X)} \| Z^{(j_1)}_{P, C} \|_X \cdots \| Z^{(j_k)}_{P, C} \|_X\\ & \trianglelefteqslant & \| C \|_X + \lambda \mathfrak{P} (S_{Z_{P, C}} (\lambda)) \end{eqnarray*}

where we used inequality (104) and the fact that, by Theorem 93, the function \(S_{P, C}\) is real analytic in a neighborhood of \(0\) with positive derivatives in \(0\). By Lemma 89 the thesis follows by taking \(G_{g, c} (a, \lambda) = \lambda \mathfrak{P} (a) + \| C \|_X\).\(\Box\)

Proof of Theorem 83

. We want to apply the previous theorems for proving Theorem 83. Here the Banach space \(X\) is \(X = C^0 (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{C}^q (\mathbb{R}^2, \mathcal{A} \otimes \mathbb{C}^4))\) with the norm given by

\(\displaystyle \| \tilde{\Psi} \|_X = \sup_{t \in \mathbb{R}} \| \tilde{\Psi} \|_{\mathcal{C}^q (\mathbb{R}^2, \mathcal{A} \otimes \mathbb{C}^4)} .\)

The function \(Z_{P_N (F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}), \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}} (\tilde{\lambda}) \) is real analytic in \(\tilde{\lambda}\) in its radius of convergence. Furthermore it is a solution to equation (94), which is of the form (102) with

\(\displaystyle P_{3, h_R, N} (\tilde{\Psi}) (t, \cdot) = \int_{- \infty}^t e^{(\Delta - m_f^2) (t - \tau)} P_N (F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y} (\tilde{\Psi} (\tau, \cdot))) \mathrm{d} \tau, \) (105)

\(C = \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}\), and \(\mathfrak{P}_{h_R, N} (\tilde{\lambda}) = \| P_{3, h_R, N} \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^3, X)} \tilde{\lambda}^3\). This means that, by Theorem 96, we have

\(\displaystyle Z_{P_N (F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}), \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P_{3, h_R, N}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R}},\)

where\(\) \(K_{P_{3, h, N}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R}}\) is the solution to the equation

\(\displaystyle K_{P_{3, h_R, N}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R}} (\tilde{\lambda}) = \tilde{\lambda} \| P_{3, h_R, N} \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^3, X)} \left( K_{P_{3, h_R, N}, {\tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R}}_X} (\tilde{\lambda}) \right)^3 + \| \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R} \|_X .\)

Since \(\| P_{3, h_R, N} \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^3, X)} \leqslant \| P_{3, h_R} \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^3, X)}\) (i.e. the polynomial \(P_{3, h_R, N}\) without the finite dimensional projection), by Remark 90, we have \(Z_{P_N (F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}), \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P_{3, h_R}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R}}\). Since \(Z_{P_N (F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}), \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}}\) converges point-wise to \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}}\) (i.e. the solution to equation (94) without the finite dimensional projection in the non-linearity) we have that, by Theorem 94, \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}}\) is real analytic and \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P_{3, h_R}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R}}\).

Using again Remark 90 and the fact that \(\| \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t} \| \leqslant \| \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t} \|_X\) (where, we recall, that \(\tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}\) is the periodized version of \(\tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}\)), we get \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P_{3, h_R}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R}} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P_{3, h}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon}}\). Furthermore, since \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h_R, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}}\) converges to \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}}\) (where \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}}\) is the solution to equation (94) where \(\tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}\) is replaced by the non-periodized version \(\tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}\) of \(\tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, R, t}\)) in \(X' = C^0 (\mathbb{R}, \mathcal{C}^q_{\ell} (\mathbb{R}^2, \mathcal{A} \otimes \mathbb{C}^4)) \supset X\) (where we consider the weighted space \(\mathcal{C}^q_{\ell}\) instead of the unweighted one), by Theorem 94 and Remark 95, we obtain that \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}}\) is real analytic with respect to \(\tilde{\lambda}\) and also \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P_{3, h}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon}}\). The thesis follows in a similar way, using the fact that, by Remark 90 and the fact that \(\| P_{3, h} \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^3, X)} \leqslant \| P_3 \|_{\mathcal{L} (X^3, X)}\) (where \(P_3\) is the nonlinearity defined in equation (105) where all the cut-offs have been removed), we have \(K_{P_{3, h}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon}} \trianglelefteqslant K_{P_3, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon}}\), and the convergence \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, h, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}}\) to \(Z_{F_{\varepsilon, Y}, \tilde{B}^A_{\varepsilon, t}}\) (i.e. the solution to equation (93)).\(\Box\)
